At work, even during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—a pump vibrating, a student making some side comment, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m gone, chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing real problems now. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself, because I’m missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And with supervising people in the lab, it’s gotten strained enough that they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations. It’s stressful, and honestly embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get overly excited or enthusiastic about the things that distract you?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is almost electrified. I get really caught up in whatever idea or detail grabs me. But if someone questions the plan or tells me to slow down, I snap at them. It’s gotten bad enough that some of the students avoid coming to my office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way and get distracted when you're not at work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work, because that’s where it causes the biggest mess. Like I said, I’ll be in a grant call or safety meeting and some tiny thing grabs me—a pump vibration, a side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing it for hours. The actual experiment, or whatever people needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still, like in meetings or when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sitting still feels almost impossible sometimes. I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, already rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once. It feels like if I sit down and slow down, I’m wasting the chance to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: It can happen outside work too, but the clearest problem is at the lab. That’s actually why I came in — I can’t keep my mind on the experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and suddenly a pump vibration, or something a student says, or some weird old spectrum peak just pulls me away. Then I’m chasing that for hours, and the thing everyone needed me to do falls apart.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It feels like if I sit down and slow down, I’m wasting the chance to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: It can happen outside work too, but the clearest problem is at the lab. That’s actually why I came in — I can’t keep my mind on the experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and suddenly a pump vibration, or something a student says, or some weird old spectrum peak just pulls me away. Then I’m chasing that for hours, and the thing everyone needed me to do falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel distracted or restless, even at work?\n\nPatient: Not lately, no. At work it’s been pretty constant. I’ll try to get through a grant call or a safety meeting, and then something small—like a pump vibration, a comment from a student, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—just grabs my attention. Then I’m off on that for hours, and the actual experiment I’m supposed to be handling falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off a lot, but I might not be noticing it. If I do it, no one has really called me out on it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have your relationships with friends or family members been affected by your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: It’s affected work relationships more than family, honestly. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities or gotten scheduling confused. And with the people I supervise, it’s gotten strained. It’s at the point where they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations, which is… hard to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. At work, even during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—a pump vibrating, a student making some side comment, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m gone, chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing real problems now. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself, because I’m missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And with supervising people in the lab, it’s gotten strained enough that they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations. It’s stressful, and honestly embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get overly excited or enthusiastic about the things that distract you?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is almost electrified. I get really caught up in whatever idea or detail grabs me. But if someone questions the plan or tells me to slow down, I snap at them. It’s gotten bad enough that some of the students avoid coming to my office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way and get distracted when you're not at work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work, because that’s where it causes the biggest mess. Like I said, I’ll be in a grant call or safety meeting and some tiny thing grabs me—a pump vibration, a side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing it for hours. The actual experiment, or whatever people needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still, like in meetings or when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sitting still feels almost impossible sometimes. I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, already rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once. It feels like if I sit down and slow down, I’m wasting the chance to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: It can happen outside work too, but the clearest problem is at the lab. That’s actually why I came in — I can’t keep my mind on the experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and suddenly a pump vibration, or something a student says, or some weird old spectrum peak just pulls me away. Then I’m chasing that for hours, and the thing everyone needed me to do falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel distracted or restless, even at work?\n\nPatient: Not lately, no. At work it’s been pretty constant. I’ll try to get through a grant call or a safety meeting, and then something small—like a pump vibration, a comment from a student, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—just grabs my attention. Then I’m off on that for hours, and the actual experiment I’m supposed to be handling falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off a lot, but I might not be noticing it. If I do it, no one has really called me out on it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have your relationships with friends or family members been affected by your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: It’s affected work relationships more than family, honestly. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities or gotten scheduling confused. And with the people I supervise, it’s gotten strained. It’s at the point where they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations, which is… hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant mood swings, where you feel extremely happy or irritable one moment and then suddenly feel down or normal the next?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, almost like everything is charged up. But then if someone questions my plan or tells me to slow down, I can get irritated really fast. I’ve snapped at students over it, enough that some of them seem to avoid coming to my office now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and distraction?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It really started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, though it’s been more obvious at work than with friends or family. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle because I’ve been missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And the supervision part has gotten strained too… enough that people are worried it could affect the project and even lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty managing your daily life at home due to these distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home, it’s not as clear to me, but the problems are definitely spilling into daily functioning. At work, my department has had to step in on decisions I normally handle because I’ve been missing priorities and confusing the schedule. The supervision side has gotten strained too, and people are worried it could affect the project and lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with chores at home the way I’ve noticed things going wrong at work. At home it’s harder for me to tell, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t keep my mind on what I’m supposed to be doing anymore. At work, even during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something small—a pump vibrating, a student making some side comment, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m gone, chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the actual experiment, or the thing everyone needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Are these distractions causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s causing real problems now. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself, because I’m missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And with supervising people in the lab, it’s gotten strained enough that they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations. It’s stressful, and honestly embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get overly excited or enthusiastic about the things that distract you?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is almost electrified. I get really caught up in whatever idea or detail grabs me. But if someone questions the plan or tells me to slow down, I snap at them. It’s gotten bad enough that some of the students avoid coming to my office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way and get distracted when you're not at work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work, because that’s where it causes the biggest mess. Like I said, I’ll be in a grant call or safety meeting and some tiny thing grabs me—a pump vibration, a side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing it for hours. The actual experiment, or whatever people needed from me, just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still, like in meetings or when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sitting still feels almost impossible sometimes. I’m at the lab before anyone else gets there, already rewriting protocols, moving instrument schedules around, emailing collaborators in different countries, and starting new analyses all at once. It feels like if I sit down and slow down, I’m wasting the chance to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: It can happen outside work too, but the clearest problem is at the lab. That’s actually why I came in — I can’t keep my mind on the experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and suddenly a pump vibration, or something a student says, or some weird old spectrum peak just pulls me away. Then I’m chasing that for hours, and the thing everyone needed me to do falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel distracted or restless, even at work?\n\nPatient: Not lately, no. At work it’s been pretty constant. I’ll try to get through a grant call or a safety meeting, and then something small—like a pump vibration, a comment from a student, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—just grabs my attention. Then I’m off on that for hours, and the actual experiment I’m supposed to be handling falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off a lot, but I might not be noticing it. If I do it, no one has really called me out on it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have your relationships with friends or family members been affected by your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: It’s affected work relationships more than family, honestly. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities or gotten scheduling confused. And with the people I supervise, it’s gotten strained. It’s at the point where they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations, which is… hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant mood swings, where you feel extremely happy or irritable one moment and then suddenly feel down or normal the next?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, almost like everything is charged up. But then if someone questions my plan or tells me to slow down, I can get irritated really fast. I’ve snapped at students over it, enough that some of them seem to avoid coming to my office now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and distraction?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It really started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, though it’s been more obvious at work than with friends or family. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle because I’ve been missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And the supervision part has gotten strained too… enough that people are worried it could affect the project and even lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty managing your daily life at home due to these distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home, it’s not as clear to me, but the problems are definitely spilling into daily functioning. At work, my department has had to step in on decisions I normally handle because I’ve been missing priorities and confusing the schedule. The supervision side has gotten strained too, and people are worried it could affect the project and lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with chores at home the way I’ve noticed things going wrong at work. At home it’s harder for me to tell, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships with friends or family members due to your behavior?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change with friends or family. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me the way other problems have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels like if I sit down and slow down, I’m wasting the chance to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: It can happen outside work too, but the clearest problem is at the lab. That’s actually why I came in — I can’t keep my mind on the experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting and suddenly a pump vibration, or something a student says, or some weird old spectrum peak just pulls me away. Then I’m chasing that for hours, and the thing everyone needed me to do falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel distracted or restless, even at work?\n\nPatient: Not lately, no. At work it’s been pretty constant. I’ll try to get through a grant call or a safety meeting, and then something small—like a pump vibration, a comment from a student, or some weird peak in an old spectrum—just grabs my attention. Then I’m off on that for hours, and the actual experiment I’m supposed to be handling falls apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who cuts people off a lot, but I might not be noticing it. If I do it, no one has really called me out on it directly.\n\nDoctor: Have your relationships with friends or family members been affected by your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: It’s affected work relationships more than family, honestly. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself because I’ve missed priorities or gotten scheduling confused. And with the people I supervise, it’s gotten strained. It’s at the point where they’re worried about the project and even lab safety expectations, which is… hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant mood swings, where you feel extremely happy or irritable one moment and then suddenly feel down or normal the next?\n\nPatient: Yes. For months I’ve had this feeling like the lab is finally moving at my speed, almost like everything is charged up. But then if someone questions my plan or tells me to slow down, I can get irritated really fast. I’ve snapped at students over it, enough that some of them seem to avoid coming to my office now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of restlessness and distraction?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It really started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your behavior or distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, though it’s been more obvious at work than with friends or family. My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle because I’ve been missing priorities and mixing up scheduling. And the supervision part has gotten strained too… enough that people are worried it could affect the project and even lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty managing your daily life at home due to these distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: At home, it’s not as clear to me, but the problems are definitely spilling into daily functioning. At work, my department has had to step in on decisions I normally handle because I’ve been missing priorities and confusing the schedule. The supervision side has gotten strained too, and people are worried it could affect the project and lab safety expectations.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your distractions and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a specific problem with chores at home the way I’ve noticed things going wrong at work. At home it’s harder for me to tell, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships with friends or family members due to your behavior?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a clear change with friends or family. If there is something there, it hasn’t stood out to me the way other problems have.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of distraction and restlessness for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been about seven months now, since I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity for at least six months consistently across different settings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been going on for months, and it’s not just one place. At work especially, I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Even in a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice something like a pump vibration, or a student’s side comment, or some weird peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just kind of falls apart.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
